The authors successfully translate the guidance from the diffusion model into a series of pose refinement steps. The integration of ICP for rigid transformations and the push-away strategy is a smart addition that enhances the practical performance of the system. The application to both 3D part assembly and 2D image reassembly is a nice touch that further demonstrates the potential of the approach.

**Significance:**  3D part assembly is a challenging problem with significant applications in robotics and automation. The reliance on labeled data has historically been a major bottleneck. A successful zero-shot approach would reduce the data requirements, making assembly tasks more scalable and adaptable to new environments and object types. The demonstrated performance gains over baseline methods in zero-shot settings, and even against some supervised approaches, is a significant achievement that suggests the potential to reduce the need for manual labeling in 3D assembly tasks.

**Strengths:**
*   **Novel approach:** The core concept is fresh and promising.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The authors convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach through quantitative comparisons against multiple baselines, including supervised ones.
*   **Theoretical justification:** The connection to an ICP-like process provides a theoretical foundation for the method.
*   **Push-away strategy:** The explicit collision handling method is a practical improvement over implicit methods.
*   **Generalizability:** Demonstration on airplane assembly and 2D image reassembly supports generalizability beyond chairs.
*   **Ablation studies:** Ablation studies help to understand the contributions of individual components (e.g. push-action).
*   **Well written:** The paper is clearly presented and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependence on Diffusion Models:** The performance is inherently tied to the quality and domain coverage of the pre-trained diffusion model. If the diffusion model is not well trained on the target objects, it would negatively impact the assembly process. This dependency limits the generalizability to unseen objects.
*   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The method requires careful tuning of the parameters involved in the noise addition, denoising, and ICP steps. While the authors mention setting 'z' to a small value (2 or 4), the exact values and sensitivity analysis are not exhaustively investigated.
*   **Scalability to Complex Assemblies:** While the authors showcase more complex assemblies with larger numbers of parts, the performance degradation is evident as the part count increases. Overcoming this scaling issue will require more sophisticated strategies for part relationship modeling and search.
*   **Limited Comparison to SOTA:** The baselines used for comparison, while relevant, do not represent the absolute state-of-the-art in fully supervised 3D part assembly. This comparison to SOTA in the supervised setting is important for understanding the trade-off between requiring labeled data and achieving higher accuracy.
*   **Qualitative failures:** While impressive, the qualitative results also showcase failure cases (e.g., incorrect placement of legs) indicating limitations. The discussions of failures are honest but provide limited avenues for fixing these shortcomings.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a genuinely innovative approach to a challenging problem with strong experimental support. However, it is not without its limitations in scalability, parameter sensitivity, and dependency on diffusion models. Given the novelty, demonstrated potential, and clear presentation, balanced by the mentioned weaknesses and areas for future work, I believe the paper deserves a strong score. The impact on the field is likely to be significant, spurring more research into diffusion model-guided assembly and zero-shot approaches.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Distributed Retrieval-Augmented Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00443v1)
- **Authors**: Chenhao Xu, Longxiang Gao, Yuan Miao, Xi Zheng
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly adopted on edge devices, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is gaining prominence as a solution to address factual deficiencies and hallucinations by integrating external knowledge. However, centralized RAG architectures face significant challenges in data privacy and scalability. For instance, smart healthcare services often rely on collecting sensitive patient data and building a centralized knowledge base to provide better diagnosis and treatment advice, while privacy concerns significantly impede this process. Besides, maintaining a comprehensive and continuously updated knowledge base is costly, particularly in response to regional epidemics and rapidly mutating viruses. To address these challenges, this paper introduces Distributed Retrieval-Augmented Generation (DRAG), a novel framework that improves data privacy by eliminating the need for a centralized knowledge base and restoring data control to owners. DRAG incorporates a Topic-Aware Random Walk (TARW) algorithm that leverages LLMs to extract query topics and facilitate targeted peer discovery within a peer-to-peer network, enabling efficient knowledge retrieval in decentralized environments. Extensive experiments across three diverse datasets and LLMs demonstrate that DRAG with TARW achieves near-centralized RAG performance by using half as many messages as flooding. The code is available at https://github.com/xuchenhao001/DRAG.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Distributed Retrieval-Augmented Generation."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Distributed Retrieval-Augmented Generation (DRAG) as a framework to address the data privacy and scalability limitations of centralized RAG architectures. Instead of relying on a central knowledge base, DRAG distributes knowledge and computation across a peer-to-peer (P2P) network. It proposes a Topic-Aware Random Walk (TARW) algorithm that leverages LLMs to extract query topics and guide peer discovery within the P2P network, facilitating efficient knowledge retrieval. The authors conduct experiments on multiple datasets and LLMs, demonstrating that DRAG with TARW can achieve performance comparable to centralized RAG with significantly reduced communication overhead compared to a flooding approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of distributed RAG is novel and addresses a significant practical challenge for edge computing applications where data privacy is paramount. Shifting away from centralized knowledge bases in RAG is a substantial departure from the existing literature. The proposed TARW algorithm builds upon standard random walks by incorporating semantic understanding of queries. This is an incremental advance, but well-motivated by the application context.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from its potential impact on enabling LLM-powered applications in privacy-sensitive domains like healthcare and autonomous driving. Decentralizing RAG could unlock use cases previously hindered by regulations and security concerns. The empirical results demonstrate the feasibility of this approach, although they are conducted in a simulated environment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained framework and algorithm.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with diverse datasets and LLMs.
    *   Strong empirical results, demonstrating comparable performance to centralized RAG with lower communication costs.
    *   Addresses a timely and relevant challenge in the age of LLMs and edge computing.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The evaluation is primarily conducted in a simulated environment. While the Barabási-Albert model mimics real-world network topologies, the simulation might not fully capture the complexities of real-world P2P networks (e.g., node churn, heterogeneous network conditions, malicious actors).

    *   The "distributed retrieval" phase, relies on peers sharing "user-filtered knowledge snippets". The methods to perform the knowledge filtering might not be robust to information leakage. The paper acknowledges the need of further privacy considerations, such as differential privacy or access control mechanisms.

    *   The evaluation focuses on performance metrics (EM, F1, etc.) and communication overhead but lacks a deeper analysis of other practical aspects, such as fault tolerance and incentive mechanisms for knowledge contribution, which are discussed in detail.
    *   The "scalability" analysis focuses mostly on the number of nodes. A discussion on the "size" of the knowledge base within the nodes, or how to distribute topics within peers could have been added.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper provides a solid foundation for future research on distributed RAG. It could inspire further investigation into alternative routing algorithms, privacy-enhancing techniques, incentive mechanisms, and real-world deployments. The TARW algorithm could also be adapted for other P2P search applications beyond RAG.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

While the paper is well-written and demonstrates a promising approach, it suffers from limitations in its evaluation and scope. The simulation results are encouraging, but the lack of real-world experimentation hinders a full assessment of its practical viability. Furthermore, several aspects of the design -- the design of user knowledge filters, and the need for incentive mechanisms -- are discussed, but not evaluated. Addressing some of these points may increase the value of this work significantly.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Data Therapist: Eliciting Domain Knowledge from Subject Matter Experts Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00455v1)
- **Authors**: Sungbok Shin, Hyeon Jeon, Sanghyun Hong, Niklas Elmqvist
- **Abstract**: Effective data visualization requires not only technical proficiency but also a deep understanding of the domain-specific context in which data exists. This context often includes tacit knowledge about data provenance, quality, and intended use, which is rarely explicit in the dataset itself. We present the Data Therapist, a web-based tool that helps domain experts externalize this implicit knowledge through a mixed-initiative process combining iterative Q&A with interactive annotation. Powered by a large language model, the system analyzes user-supplied datasets, prompts users with targeted questions, and allows annotation at varying levels of granularity. The resulting structured knowledge base can inform both human and automated visualization design. We evaluated the tool in a qualitative study involving expert pairs from Molecular Biology, Accounting, Political Science, and Usable Security. The study revealed recurring patterns in how experts reason about their data and highlights areas where AI support can improve visualization design.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Data Therapist: Eliciting Domain Knowledge from Subject Matter Experts Using Large Language Models."

**Summary**

The paper introduces Data Therapist, a web-based tool designed to elicit domain knowledge from subject matter experts (SMEs) about datasets using a mixed-initiative approach. The system utilizes a large language model (LLM) to analyze user-provided datasets, generate targeted questions based on metadata categories, and enable interactive annotation at various levels of granularity (data points, columns, rows, overall dataset). Data Therapist aims to bridge the gap between the raw data and real-world context by capturing tacit knowledge that often exists only in the minds of domain experts. This knowledge is structured into a knowledge base that can inform human or automated visualization design processes. The paper presents a qualitative user study involving expert pairs from diverse fields (Molecular Biology, Accounting, Political Science, and Usable Security) demonstrating that Data Therapist can effectively capture domain knowledge and elicit user insight in a user-friendly approach.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs to guide domain knowledge elicitation for visualization design is relatively novel.  While LLMs have been used for various tasks related to data analysis and generation, their application to systematically extracting tacit knowledge *specifically* for visualization purposes represents a significant contribution. The integration of interactive annotation with LLM-driven questioning is another novel aspect, providing a structured yet flexible environment for experts to externalize their knowledge. Prior work primarily focused on either manual knowledge elicitation or automated recommendation systems without the crucial element of interactive, domain-aware guidance. Data Therapist brings LLM's capability to the problem of understanding data from a domain expert's viewpoint.

**Significance:** The significance of this work lies in addressing the well-known challenge of knowledge transfer between domain experts and visualization designers.  The communication gap often leads to visualizations that are technically sound but fail to capture the nuances and context crucial for effective interpretation and decision-making. Data Therapist offers a potential solution by providing a structured and accessible way for experts to share their insights.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a Real Problem:** The paper tackles a significant problem in the visualization community – the difficulty of acquiring and incorporating domain-specific knowledge into visualization design.
*   **Mixed-Initiative Approach:** The combination of LLM-driven questioning and user-driven annotation is a key strength. It allows the system to proactively guide the elicitation process while giving experts the flexibility to contribute their own insights.
*   **Comprehensive Metadata Coverage:** The paper's grounding in Gebru et al.'s (2021) metadata framework ensures that the elicitation process is systematic and covers all relevant aspects of the dataset context.
*   **Qualitative Evaluation:** The qualitative user study provides valuable insights into how experts interact with the tool and the types of knowledge they are able to externalize.
*   **Practical Implementation:** The tool is functional and available, enhancing the potential for adoption and further development by the research community.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Qualitative Study Limited Scope:** While the qualitative study provides valuable insights, the sample size is relatively small (8 participants). A larger, more quantitative study would be necessary to further validate the effectiveness and usability of the system. There are also potential biases from participant expertise and computer literacy.
*   **LLM Dependency and Cost:** The system's reliance on an external LLM service like GPT-4 raises concerns about cost, scalability, and potential biases in the LLM itself.  Exploring open-source LLMs or fine-tuning LLMs on domain-specific data could be avenues for future research.
*   **Evaluation Metric:** While the study evaluates informativeness and difficulty of the annotations, it does not directly measure the impact of the elicited knowledge on visualization design outcomes. A more rigorous evaluation would involve comparing visualizations created with and without the use of Data Therapist.
*   **Limited Focus on Visualization Outcome:** The paper primarily focuses on knowledge elicitation, with limited discussion on how the structured knowledge base is actually used to inform visualization design. The connection between the output of Data Therapist and the creation of visualizations is a missing link in the research narrative.
*   **Overclaims in Conclusion:** In the discussion, the study's conclusion about how LLM falls short in guiding the annotation could not be firmly drawn as most participants noted that "the predefined questions were not particularly relevant to what they were trying to do."
*   **The System as a Black Box:** It is unclear on the process to generate questions, or to determine an order of priority. More information to expose inner processes would be beneficial.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Encouraging researchers to explore the use of LLMs for knowledge management in visualization design.
*   Providing a practical tool and framework for systematically eliciting domain knowledge from experts.
*   Highlighting the importance of bridging the gap between data and real-world context in visualization design.

**Justification of Score**

I assign a score of **7.5**. The Data Therapist provides a novel and practical approach to a persistent problem in the visualization community. However, the limited scope of the user study, LLM reliance, and absence of an evaluation of the visualization outcomes constrain the score from going higher. Despite the flaws, the system is implemented, offers a clear framework, addresses an ongoing challenge, and provides a useful starting point for additional research.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Red Teaming Large Language Models for Healthcare
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00467v1)
- **Authors**: Vahid Balazadeh, Michael Cooper, David Pellow, Atousa Assadi, Jennifer Bell, Jim Fackler, Gabriel Funingana, Spencer Gable-Cook, Anirudh Gangadhar, Abhishek Jaiswal, Sumanth Kaja, Christopher Khoury, Randy Lin, Kaden McKeen, Sara Naimimohasses, Khashayar Namdar, Aviraj Newatia, Allan Pang, Anshul Pattoo, Sameer Peesapati, Diana Prepelita, Bogdana Rakova, Saba Sadatamin, Rafael Schulman, Ajay Shah, Syed Azhar Shah, Syed Ahmar Shah, Babak Taati, Balagopal Unnikrishnan, Stephanie Williams, Rahul G Krishnan
- **Abstract**: We present the design process and findings of the pre-conference workshop at the Machine Learning for Healthcare Conference (2024) entitled Red Teaming Large Language Models for Healthcare, which took place on August 15, 2024. Conference participants, comprising a mix of computational and clinical expertise, attempted to discover vulnerabilities -- realistic clinical prompts for which a large language model (LLM) outputs a response that could cause clinical harm. Red-teaming with clinicians enables the identification of LLM vulnerabilities that may not be recognised by LLM developers lacking clinical expertise. We report the vulnerabilities found, categorise them, and present the results of a replication study assessing the vulnerabilities across all LLMs provided.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper details a workshop focused on red-teaming large language models (LLMs) for healthcare applications. Participants, including clinicians and computational experts, collaborated to identify vulnerabilities in LLMs when presented with realistic clinical prompts. The study catalogued various harmful responses, categorized them into areas such as hallucination, image interpretation failure, anchoring bias, and incorrect medical knowledge.  A replication study was performed across different LLM models to assess the persistence of these vulnerabilities over time and across different LLMs. The work underscores the importance of clinician involvement in identifying LLM limitations that developers without clinical expertise might miss.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clinician-Driven Red-Teaming:** The core strength lies in its reliance on clinical expertise to formulate realistic prompts and evaluate LLM responses. This contrasts with many red-teaming efforts primarily focused on adversarial attacks from a computer science perspective.
    *   **Practical Vulnerability Identification:** The paper identifies tangible and clinically relevant vulnerabilities. This is more valuable than abstract theoretical concerns.
    *   **Vulnerability Categorization:** The classification of vulnerabilities provides a framework for understanding potential LLM failure modes in healthcare, offering guidance to developers and users.
    *   **Replication Study:** The longitudinal element with the replication study highlighting the dynamic nature of the problem. Shows vulnerabilities are not static and models change over time so continuous evaluation is key.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Selection Bias:** The reliance on volunteer workshop participants may introduce a selection bias. While diverse, the representation of all clinical specialties might be limited. Participants favoured using ChatGPT which introduced a bias in testing.
    *   **Reporting Bias:** Data collection emphasized reporting vulnerabilities rather than documenting adequate responses, creating an incomplete picture of overall LLM performance. There's no insight into the prevalence of harmful responses versus the proportion of generally useful or accurate responses.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** Results might be specific to the tested models and prompts. Rapid LLM evolution means these vulnerabilities could become obsolete or be superseded by new types of errors.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The number of prompts are limited, and certain prompt types/scenarios may not have been captured.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution by:

*   **Shifting focus:** Moving from adversarial red-teaming to clinically-grounded vulnerability assessment with realistic cases.
*   **Highlighting dynamic nature:** emphasizing the evolutionary nature of LLM performance, thus requiring ongoing monitoring.
*   **Establishing a framework** Creating a taxonomy of failure categories that is easily followed.
*   **Emphasis on clinician involvement:** Highlighting the importance of clinicians for clinical settings and cases.
The paper's influence will likely be in informing red-teaming protocols and encouraging more collaboration between technical experts and healthcare professionals. However, the limited scope tempers its long-term impact.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign this paper a score of **7**.

*   **Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable, practically-oriented study with a good balance of theoretical framework and empirical findings. The reliance on clinical expertise is commendable. However, the limitations of the sample, the reporting bias, and the potential for rapid obsolescence temper the long-term significance. The work is likely to be influential within the specialized field of LLM evaluation in healthcare, but lacks the breadth and depth to qualify as a groundbreaking contribution to the broader AI field. It is a well executed, focused study that identifies real-world problems, which has a significance but is limited in generalisability.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Interpretable Spatial-Temporal Fusion Transformers: Multi-Output Prediction for Parametric Dynamical Systems with Time-Varying Inputs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00473v1)
- **Authors**: Shuwen Sun, Lihong Feng, Peter Benner
- **Abstract**: We explore the promising performance of a transformer model in predicting outputs of parametric dynamical systems with external time-varying input signals. The outputs of such systems vary not only with physical parameters but also with external time-varying input signals. Accurately catching the dynamics of such systems is challenging. We have adapted and extended an existing transformer model for single output prediction to a multiple-output transformer that is able to predict multiple output responses of these systems. The multiple-output transformer generalizes the interpretability of the original transformer. The generalized interpretable attention weight matrix explores not only the temporal correlations in the sequence, but also the interactions between the multiple outputs, providing explanation for the spatial correlation in the output domain. This multiple-output transformer accurately predicts the sequence of multiple outputs, regardless of the nonlinearity of the system and the dimensionality of the parameter space.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Interpretable Spatial-Temporal Fusion Transformers: Multi-Output Prediction for Parametric Dynamical Systems with Time-Varying Inputs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Interpretable Spatial-Temporal Fusion Transformer (iSTFT), an extension of the Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT), to predict multiple outputs (Quantities of Interest - QoIs) of parametric dynamical systems with time-varying inputs. The original TFT is designed for predicting a single output and needs retraining for each new QoI. The iSTFT overcomes this limitation by predicting multiple outputs simultaneously.  The key innovations are a new masking scheme for the multi-head attention mechanism to capture spatial correlations (relationships between outputs) in addition to temporal correlations, and a reshaping of the input data to facilitate this. The authors demonstrate iSTFT's effectiveness and interpretability on three dynamical systems: the Lorenz-63 model, the FitzHugh-Nagumo model, and a Ferrocyanide oxidation reaction model. They analyze the attention weights and variable importance to gain insights into the system dynamics and relationships between the outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Extension of TFT:**  The core idea of adapting TFT for multi-output prediction is a logical extension, but the specific implementation choices, particularly the masking scheme for the multi-head attention to incorporate spatial dependencies, provide some degree of novelty.
*   **Spatial-Temporal Attention:** Introducing spatial relationships between outputs into the attention mechanism is a good direction. This enables the model to capture dependencies that a simple temporal model would miss.

*   **Limited Methodological Innovation:**  The fundamental architecture remains based on TFT. The modifications are primarily focused on data pre-processing and masking, which while effective, aren't groundbreaking architectural innovations in the transformer space.

**Significance:**

*   **Multi-Output Prediction for Dynamical Systems:**  The ability to predict multiple QoIs simultaneously with a single model is practically useful, reducing training overhead and improving efficiency.
*   **Interpretability:**  The preservation and extension of interpretability from TFT to iSTFT is valuable. Being able to analyze the attention weights provides insights into system dynamics, making the model more trustworthy, and aiding in scientific discovery.
*   **Applicability to Parametric Systems:**  The focus on parametric dynamical systems with time-varying inputs makes the work relevant to various engineering and scientific applications where such systems are common.
*   **Limited Breadth of Evaluation:** While the use cases provided are helpful, the Ferrocyanide oxidation reaction model equations are not directly presented in the manuscript. It only mentions the ODE for the charge balance and the Butler-Volmer kinetic equations. The simulation parameters for each experiment are listed in the paper, which makes the experiments more verifiable. However, the number of parameter samples for Ferrocyanide oxidation reaction model is only np = 450, which is the smallest sample for training the iSTFT comparing to other numerical examples.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem definition: The paper addresses a relevant limitation of the original TFT model.
*   Well-defined modifications: The proposed changes to TFT are well-explained and justified.
*   Empirical validation: The performance on multiple benchmark dynamical systems provides evidence for the effectiveness of iSTFT.
*   Interpretability analysis: The analysis of attention weights and variable importance provides valuable insights.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:**  The core architecture is largely borrowed from TFT. The main innovations are in data manipulation and masking, which may not be seen as substantial contributions by some.
*   **Limited Comparisons:**  The paper could benefit from stronger comparisons to other multi-output time series forecasting methods specifically designed for dynamical systems (if such methods exist), beyond simply stating the benefits of iSTFT.
*   **No code repository** There is no link to a code repository to verify the methods used to obtain the results.

**Potential Influence:**

*   The iSTFT could become a useful tool for researchers and practitioners working with parametric dynamical systems, especially in scenarios where multiple outputs need to be predicted efficiently.
*   The ideas presented in the paper could inspire further research into incorporating spatial dependencies in attention mechanisms for other multi-output forecasting tasks.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed extension of an existing transformer model for a practically relevant problem. The novelty lies in the spatial-temporal attention mechanism and the specific implementation for multi-output prediction in dynamical systems. The paper provides solid empirical validation and demonstrates the value of interpretability. However, the overall novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary, and the breadth of comparisons could be improved. Also the lack of open-sourced code limits the impact of the research because other researchers could not verify the methods used to obtain the results. For these reasons, I believe a score of 6 is appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### JointDiT: Enhancing RGB-Depth Joint Modeling with Diffusion Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00482v1)
- **Authors**: Kwon Byung-Ki, Qi Dai, Lee Hyoseok, Chong Luo, Tae-Hyun Oh
- **Abstract**: We present JointDiT, a diffusion transformer that models the joint distribution of RGB and depth. By leveraging the architectural benefit and outstanding image prior of the state-of-the-art diffusion transformer, JointDiT not only generates high-fidelity images but also produces geometrically plausible and accurate depth maps. This solid joint distribution modeling is achieved through two simple yet effective techniques that we propose, i.e., adaptive scheduling weights, which depend on the noise levels of each modality, and the unbalanced timestep sampling strategy. With these techniques, we train our model across all noise levels for each modality, enabling JointDiT to naturally handle various combinatorial generation tasks, including joint generation, depth estimation, and depth-conditioned image generation by simply controlling the timestep of each branch. JointDiT demonstrates outstanding joint generation performance. Furthermore, it achieves comparable results in depth estimation and depth-conditioned image generation, suggesting that joint distribution modeling can serve as a replaceable alternative to conditional generation. The project page is available at https://byungki-k.github.io/JointDiT/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces JointDiT, a diffusion transformer model for jointly modeling RGB images and depth maps.  It leverages the architecture and image prior of diffusion transformers to generate high-fidelity images and geometrically plausible depth maps. Two key techniques are proposed: adaptive scheduling weights, which adjust based on the noise levels of each modality, and an unbalanced timestep sampling strategy. These techniques enable JointDiT to handle various tasks, including joint generation, depth estimation, and depth-conditioned image generation, by simply controlling the timestep of each branch. The paper demonstrates that JointDiT achieves strong joint generation performance and comparable results in depth estimation and depth-conditioned image generation compared to existing conditional generation models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the specific combination of existing techniques (diffusion transformers, separate noise level training) along with the introduction of adaptive scheduling weights and the unbalanced timestep sampling strategy for the joint modeling of RGB and depth. Diffusion models have been used for image and depth before, but applying DiTs and tuning the training process specifically for joint tasks adds to the innovation.
*   **Significance:** The paper’s significance comes from its potential to provide a unified framework for various tasks related to image and depth, potentially replacing separate conditional generation models. It can perform diverse tasks like joint generation and depth estimation using a single model. High quality generation using a solid architecture and good training methodology could spark further interest in how to train DiTs for other multi-modal combinations.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of a DiT architecture seems to result in better image quality and geometric plausibility compared to models using U-Net backbones as it has a global receptive field, which could enable better modelling.
    *   The adaptive scheduling weights and unbalanced timestep sampling are simple yet effective techniques.
    *   The model is versatile and can perform joint generation, depth estimation, and depth-conditioned image generation with simple adjustments.
    *   Demonstrated performance improvements over existing joint generation methods and competitive results with depth estimation models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The techniques are incremental improvements on existing diffusion models. The core architectural idea of a parallel depth branch with cross-attention modules is fairly straightforward.
    *   The gains in depth estimation over dedicated depth estimation models appear only with model fine-tuning.
    *   The paper mentions limitations: increased batch size, additional parameters, and increased sampling time compared to a model for generating a single image. This limits scalability.
    *   The contribution does not address new tasks. It instead provides a method to execute them and replace existing methods.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging the use of diffusion transformers for multimodal tasks.
    *   Providing a foundation for exploring more sophisticated joint training strategies for diffusion models.
    *   Inspiring the development of more lightweight and efficient diffusion transformer architectures for joint modeling.
    *   Prompting investigation of other modalities or combinations to be used alongside images for generation.
    *   Spurring development of even more rigorous methods to utilize depth maps beyond the capabilities of the models shown here.

*   **Overall Assessment:** The paper presents a valuable contribution by demonstrating how diffusion transformers can be adapted for joint modeling of RGB images and depth maps. The proposed techniques are relatively simple and effective, and the results are promising. While the novelty is incremental, the potential impact on streamlining various vision tasks is significant.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HalluMix: A Task-Agnostic, Multi-Domain Benchmark for Real-World Hallucination Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00506v1)
- **Authors**: Deanna Emery, Michael Goitia, Freddie Vargus, Iulia Neagu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains, detecting hallucinated content$\unicode{x2013}$text that is not grounded in supporting evidence$\unicode{x2013}$has become a critical challenge. Existing benchmarks for hallucination detection are often synthetically generated, narrowly focused on extractive question answering, and fail to capture the complexity of real-world scenarios involving multi-document contexts and full-sentence outputs. We introduce the HalluMix Benchmark, a diverse, task-agnostic dataset that includes examples from a range of domains and formats. Using this benchmark, we evaluate seven hallucination detection systems$\unicode{x2013}$both open and closed source$\unicode{x2013}$highlighting differences in performance across tasks, document lengths, and input representations. Our analysis highlights substantial performance disparities between short and long contexts, with critical implications for real-world Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) implementations. Quotient Detections achieves the best overall performance, with an accuracy of 0.82 and an F1 score of 0.84.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "HalluMix" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HalluMix, a new benchmark dataset for evaluating hallucination detection in large language models (LLMs).  HalluMix is designed to be task-agnostic, incorporating examples from summarization, natural language inference (NLI), and question answering (QA) across diverse domains. The dataset emphasizes real-world scenarios with multi-document contexts and full-sentence outputs, addressing limitations in existing benchmarks that often rely on synthetic data or focus on single-document extractive QA. The authors evaluate seven hallucination detection systems (open and closed source) using HalluMix, highlighting performance variations based on task, content length, and input format.  Key findings indicate that existing methods exhibit overfitting to specific datasets, and that there are trade-offs between continuous-context and sentence-based approaches for handling different content lengths.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the construction of the HalluMix benchmark itself.  While previous hallucination detection datasets exist, HalluMix distinguishes itself through its task-agnostic design, multi-document context, and use of human-curated data. The dataset's focus on reflecting real-world use cases, including Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) scenarios, is valuable. However, the individual data transformations used for generating hallucinated examples are not particularly novel; they build upon existing techniques like summary mismatches, question-answer permutations, and NLI relabeling. The contribution comes from the *combination* of these and the effort to curate and balance the resulting dataset.
*   **Significance:** HalluMix has the potential to be a significant contribution to the field. The dataset addresses a recognized gap in the availability of realistic and challenging benchmarks for hallucination detection. The detailed evaluation of diverse methods, along with the analysis of their strengths and weaknesses, provides valuable insights for researchers and practitioners.  The finding that performance varies significantly based on input characteristics like content length and task is important for understanding the limitations of current techniques. The emphasis on RAG scenarios directly addresses a critical and growing area of LLM application. The release of the benchmark will also allow more systematic comparison in future research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Realism:** Addresses the shortcomings of synthetic and task-specific datasets.
    *   **Diversity:** Integrates multiple tasks and domains for broader applicability.
    *   **Multi-document Context:**  Mirrors real-world RAG implementations.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Evaluates a variety of hallucination detection methods and provides performance insights.
    * **Public Release:**  The public release of the dataset promotes further research and comparison of methodologies.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Transformation Techniques:** The individual methods for generating hallucinated examples are not revolutionary. While well-established, this could be seen as incremental compared to devising entirely new hallucination strategies.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** While the paper provides overall performance metrics, a more in-depth analysis of specific failure cases for each method could further improve the understanding of their limitations and potential directions for improvement. For example, a qualitative error analysis.
    *   **Dataset Size:** While stated as "large scale", at 6.5k examples, it may be modest relative to the largest pre-training datasets. The potential impact on model evaluations may change based on the pre-existing knowledge of LLMs.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   HalluMix has the potential to become a standard benchmark for hallucination detection, leading to more robust and reliable LLMs.
    *   The insights gained from the evaluation can guide the development of new detection methods and input handling strategies.
    *   The dataset can facilitate research on domain-specific hallucination detection and mitigation.

**Justification for Score:**

Given its innovative contribution with respect to existing frameworks and benchmarks and its potential to significantly influence the development of hallucination detection systems but its usage of existing frameworks and limited failure case analysis, a score of **7** is justifiable. HalluMix is a valuable resource that addresses a recognized need in the field, offers valuable performance insights, and establishes a foundation for future research, meriting a strong score. However, the incremental nature of the transformation techniques and opportunities for a more in-depth qualitative analysis keep it from achieving a higher level of innovation.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Self-Ablating Transformers: More Interpretability, Less Sparsity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00509v1)
- **Authors**: Jeremias Ferrao, Luhan Mikaelson, Keenan Pepper, Natalia Perez-Campanero Antolin
- **Abstract**: A growing intuition in machine learning suggests a link between sparsity and interpretability. We introduce a novel self-ablation mechanism to investigate this connection ante-hoc in the context of language transformers. Our approach dynamically enforces a k-winner-takes-all constraint, forcing the model to demonstrate selective activation across neuron and attention units. Unlike post-hoc methods that analyze already-trained models, our approach integrates interpretability directly into model training, promoting feature localization from inception. Training small models on the TinyStories dataset and employing interpretability tests, we find that self-ablation leads to more localized circuits, concentrated feature representations, and increased neuron specialization without compromising language modelling performance. Surprisingly, our method also decreased overall sparsity, indicating that self-ablation promotes specialization rather than widespread inactivity. This reveals a complex interplay between sparsity and interpretability, where decreased global sparsity can coexist with increased local specialization, leading to enhanced interpretability. To facilitate reproducibility, we make our code available at https://github.com/keenanpepper/self-ablating-transformers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Self-Ablating Transformers: More Interpretability, Less Sparsity":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel "self-ablation" mechanism for training more interpretable transformer models.  Unlike post-hoc interpretability methods, this approach integrates interpretability directly into the model training process.  It dynamically enforces a k-winner-takes-all (kWTA) constraint, encouraging selective activation across neurons and attention heads via learned gating weights. This mechanism is active only during training; at inference, the model is a standard transformer. The authors train small language models on the TinyStories dataset and use various interpretability tests (Automatic Circuit Discovery (ACDC), Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), Neuron Explainability, Neuron to Graph (N2G) analysis) to demonstrate that self-ablation leads to more localized circuits, concentrated feature representations, and increased neuron specialization *without* significantly impacting language modeling performance.  Surprisingly, the method often *decreases* overall sparsity, suggesting that focused specialization is more critical for interpretability than simply maximizing sparsity. The paper proposes a trade-off: paying a computational cost during training to achieve better interpretability at inference time, all while retaining the efficiency and interoperability of the standard transformer architecture.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The concept of using dynamic ablation during training to improve interpretability is somewhat novel, although related to techniques like codebook features and gradient routing.  The key distinction lies in its specific implementation: the use of kWTA with learned gating weights *directly* applied to neuron and attention units during training, while preserving the original transformer structure for inference.

**Significance:**

The most significant aspect of this work is its challenge to the common intuition that *increased* sparsity always leads to *increased* interpretability. The paper convincingly shows that targeted specialization, even at the expense of global sparsity, can result in more interpretable models. This is an important finding that could shift the focus of research in this area. The comprehensive evaluation using multiple interpretability metrics is also a strong point.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem statement and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the need for ante-hoc interpretability techniques in LLMs.
*   **Novel method:** The self-ablation mechanism is a well-defined and potentially useful technique.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The use of ACDC, SAEs, Neuron Explainability, and Neuron Graph analysis provides a multi-faceted view of the impact of self-ablation.
*   **Challenging established assumptions:** The counter-intuitive finding regarding sparsity is a valuable contribution.
*   **Code availability:** Encourages reproducibility and further research.
*   **Maintains inference efficiency:** This is a key advantage over some other interpretability-by-design techniques.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scale:** The experiments are conducted on small models and the TinyStories dataset. The generalizability of the findings to larger models and more complex datasets is unclear. This limits the immediate practical impact of the work.
*   **Modest improvements:** While the paper demonstrates improvements in interpretability metrics, the magnitude of these improvements is sometimes small.
*   **Increased perplexity:** Any degradation in model performance, even a small one, is a concern.
*   **Computational overhead during training:** While inference is efficient, the additional parameters for gating weights and the additional loss term for the ablated stream cause an increase in training cost. While this cost may be worth paying for the benefits of improved interpretability, the paper should more clearly quantify this trade-off.
*   **Interpretability metrics:** While multiple interpretability metrics are used, many of them rely on proxies rather than *directly* measuring model understanding.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of interpretable machine learning. The self-ablation mechanism is a novel and well-defined technique that challenges the standard view that sparsity inherently leads to interpretability. While the experiments are limited in scope, the multi-faceted evaluation provides compelling evidence that targeted specialization can indeed improve interpretability. The main limitation is the applicability to larger models and datasets. The increased perplexity and computation during training are potential drawbacks that must be weighed against the interpretability gains. Given the scope of these issues I would provide the following rating:

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Safety-Critical Traffic Simulation with Guided Latent Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00515v1)
- **Authors**: Mingxing Peng, Ruoyu Yao, Xusen Guo, Yuting Xie, Xianda Chen, Jun Ma
- **Abstract**: Safety-critical traffic simulation plays a crucial role in evaluating autonomous driving systems under rare and challenging scenarios. However, existing approaches often generate unrealistic scenarios due to insufficient consideration of physical plausibility and suffer from low generation efficiency. To address these limitations, we propose a guided latent diffusion model (LDM) capable of generating physically realistic and adversarial safety-critical traffic scenarios. Specifically, our model employs a graph-based variational autoencoder (VAE) to learn a compact latent space that captures complex multi-agent interactions while improving computational efficiency. Within this latent space, the diffusion model performs the denoising process to produce realistic trajectories. To enable controllable and adversarial scenario generation, we introduce novel guidance objectives that drive the diffusion process toward producing adversarial and behaviorally realistic driving behaviors. Furthermore, we develop a sample selection module based on physical feasibility checks to further enhance the physical plausibility of the generated scenarios. Extensive experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that our method achieves superior adversarial effectiveness and generation efficiency compared to existing baselines while maintaining a high level of realism. Our work provides an effective tool for realistic safety-critical scenario simulation, paving the way for more robust evaluation of autonomous driving systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a guided Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) for generating realistic and adversarial safety-critical traffic scenarios to evaluate autonomous driving systems.  The model uses a graph-based Variational Autoencoder (VAE) to learn a compact latent space representing multi-agent interactions. Within this space, a diffusion model reconstructs realistic trajectories. The model incorporates guidance objectives to steer the diffusion process towards adversarial behaviors and uses a sample selection module based on physical plausibility checks. Experiments on the nuScenes dataset demonstrate that the method outperforms baselines in terms of adversarial effectiveness, generation efficiency, and realism.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques (VAE, diffusion models, graph neural networks) for a specific problem: safety-critical traffic simulation.  The core novelty lies in the *specific design* of the LDM framework and, more importantly, the introduction of *novel guidance objectives* and a *sample selection module* tailored for generating *adversarial* yet physically plausible traffic scenarios. While the individual components aren't revolutionary, their integration and application to this specific domain demonstrate innovation. Compared to prior work like AdvSim and Strive, the main advantage appears to be better generation efficiency and arguably better realism (although realism metrics are always subjective).
    *   **Strengths:**  The key strength is the demonstrated improvement in generation efficiency compared to optimization-based methods like AdvSim and Strive. The tailored guidance objectives for adversarial scenarios are also a valuable contribution, as they allow for more controllable and targeted scenario generation.  The latent diffusion approach improves on direct trajectory diffusion models by enhancing computational efficiency and feature expressiveness.

    *   **Weaknesses:** The paper needs to acknowledge the limitations of the rule-based planner used for the ego vehicle, as its behavior might influence the assessment of the generated scenarios. The novelty could be further strengthened by exploring the incorporation of more sophisticated, learning-based ego vehicle planners.  The diversity metrics are decent but could benefit from a deeper analysis of the *types* of diverse scenarios generated. Are these just variations on a theme, or are they genuinely different and unexpected scenarios? Furthermore, a more detailed ablation study that breaks down the effect of each objective contributing to I(T) will provide insight into which are most effective.
    *   **Significance:** Safety-critical traffic simulation is a crucial area for developing and validating autonomous driving systems. The proposed method offers a potentially significant improvement in generation efficiency and realism, enabling faster and more robust evaluation.  The controllable adversarial generation capability is particularly valuable for identifying edge cases and vulnerabilities in autonomous driving algorithms. The work could potentially influence the development of more robust and reliable AV systems.

*   **Technical Soundness:** The experiments are well-designed and the results are clearly presented. The comparison with existing baselines is thorough, and the ablation study provides insights into the contribution of the guidance mechanism. The mathematical formulation is also clear and concise. The use of a VAE is standard practice for learning latent space representations, and this approach is well established for traffic simulation.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to impact the field by providing a more efficient and controllable approach to safety-critical traffic scenario generation. This will likely lead to more thorough and reliable evaluation of AV systems and potentially accelerate their development.

*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow. The figures are informative, and the overall structure is logical.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of safety-critical traffic simulation. It's a reasonably innovative combination of existing techniques, most notably the tailored guidance objectives and sample selection process, which together enable efficient and controllable generation of adversarial scenarios. While the individual components are not groundbreaking, their integration and specific application to this problem justify a rating of 7. The gains in efficiency relative to optimization-based methods, coupled with the improved realism, make this a significant advancement.  However, the paper's limitations regarding the rule-based planner, lack of analysis on the different objective contributions, and the potential for more in-depth diversity analysis prevent it from receiving a higher score. Future work addressing these points could further enhance the significance and impact of this research.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### 100 Days After DeepSeek-R1: A Survey on Replication Studies and More Directions for Reasoning Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00551v1)
- **Authors**: Chong Zhang, Yue Deng, Xiang Lin, Bin Wang, Dianwen Ng, Hai Ye, Xingxuan Li, Yao Xiao, Zhanfeng Mo, Qi Zhang, Lidong Bing
- **Abstract**: The recent development of reasoning language models (RLMs) represents a novel evolution in large language models. In particular, the recent release of DeepSeek-R1 has generated widespread social impact and sparked enthusiasm in the research community for exploring the explicit reasoning paradigm of language models. However, the implementation details of the released models have not been fully open-sourced by DeepSeek, including DeepSeek-R1-Zero, DeepSeek-R1, and the distilled small models. As a result, many replication studies have emerged aiming to reproduce the strong performance achieved by DeepSeek-R1, reaching comparable performance through similar training procedures and fully open-source data resources. These works have investigated feasible strategies for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR), focusing on data preparation and method design, yielding various valuable insights. In this report, we provide a summary of recent replication studies to inspire future research. We primarily focus on SFT and RLVR as two main directions, introducing the details for data construction, method design and training procedure of current replication studies. Moreover, we conclude key findings from the implementation details and experimental results reported by these studies, anticipating to inspire future research. We also discuss additional techniques of enhancing RLMs, highlighting the potential of expanding the application scope of these models, and discussing the challenges in development. By this survey, we aim to help researchers and developers of RLMs stay updated with the latest advancements, and seek to inspire new ideas to further enhance RLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a survey of recent research efforts focused on replicating and extending the capabilities of DeepSeek-R1, a reasoning language model (RLM). The survey concentrates on two primary training approaches: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR). It outlines the methodologies used for data curation, algorithm design, and training procedures in various open-source replication projects. The paper also explores alternative techniques for enhancing RLMs, including reward modeling, preference optimization, and considerations for generalizability, safety, and multimodality. The authors summarize key findings and identify promising future research directions in the field of reasoning language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary value lies in its timely and comprehensive aggregation of recent research activities surrounding DeepSeek-R1 replication. While it doesn't present fundamentally new methodologies or theoretical insights, it does offer a valuable synthesis of diverse approaches and empirical findings. The taxonomy presented in Figure 1 is a useful organization of the space.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its ability to provide a clear overview of the current state of the field, highlighting the challenges and opportunities in replicating and improving upon advanced reasoning language models. The discussion of SFT and RLVR techniques, along with the comparisons of various open-source datasets and training configurations, offers valuable guidance for researchers and practitioners. The insights regarding limitations, future directions (e.g., safety, multimodality), also contribute to shaping the research agenda. The main value is its contribution to making a relatively young subfield accessible by structuring diverse replication efforts.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:**  The survey covers a wide range of relevant papers and techniques, providing a holistic view of the field.
    *   **Clear Organization:**  The paper is well-structured, with clear sections and sub-sections that facilitate understanding.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The authors distill practical insights from the reviewed studies, offering actionable guidance for researchers.
    *   **Timeliness:** The paper addresses a rapidly evolving area of research, making it highly relevant to current interests.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Originality:** The paper primarily synthesizes existing research rather than presenting novel theoretical or methodological contributions. The authors could have strengthened the survey by, for example, analyzing the trade-offs of different approaches to a greater degree.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the paper provides a broad overview, it could benefit from a deeper analysis of specific techniques and their underlying mechanisms.
    *   **Subjectivity:**  As with any survey, the selection of papers and the interpretation of results may be influenced by the authors' perspectives. There is the usual risk that some researchers might feel that they were unfairly omitted.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to serve as a valuable reference for researchers and developers working on reasoning language models. By synthesizing current knowledge and highlighting promising research directions, it can contribute to accelerating progress in the field. It also provides a consolidated overview of the current research that helps newcomers to the field quickly develop an understanding of the research directions.

**Justification for Score:**

Given its valuable synthesis of recent research, practical insights, and timely relevance, the paper represents a solid contribution to the field. However, the limited originality and depth of analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. The field of replication efforts is still developing. The review is comprehensive.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Triggering Hallucinations in LLMs: A Quantitative Study of Prompt-Induced Hallucination in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00557v1)
- **Authors**: Makoto Sato
- **Abstract**: Hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) present a growing challenge across real-world applications, from healthcare to law, where factual reliability is essential. Despite advances in alignment and instruction tuning, LLMs can still generate outputs that are fluent yet fundamentally untrue. Understanding the cognitive dynamics that underlie these hallucinations remains an open problem. In this study, we propose a prompt-based framework to systematically trigger and quantify hallucination: a Hallucination-Inducing Prompt (HIP), which synthetically fuses semantically distant concepts (e.g., periodic table of elements and tarot divination) in a misleading way, and a Hallucination Quantifying Prompt (HQP), which scores the plausibility, confidence, and coherence of the output. Controlled experiments across multiple LLMs revealed that HIPs consistently produced less coherent and more hallucinated responses than their null-fusion controls. These effects varied across models, with reasoning-oriented LLMs showing distinct profiles from general-purpose ones. Our framework provides a reproducible testbed for studying hallucination vulnerability, and opens the door to developing safer, more introspective LLMs that can detect and self-regulate the onset of conceptual instability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Triggering Hallucinations in LLMs: A Quantitative Study of Prompt-Induced Hallucination in Large Language Models" introduces a framework for systematically triggering and quantifying hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). The framework consists of two prompt types: Hallucination-Inducing Prompts (HIPs), which fuse semantically distant concepts to mislead the model, and Hallucination Quantifying Prompts (HQPs), used to evaluate the plausibility, confidence, and coherence of the LLM's output. The authors conducted controlled experiments across several LLMs, finding that HIPs reliably induce less coherent and more hallucinated responses compared to control prompts. The degree of susceptibility to these prompts varied across models, with reasoning-oriented models exhibiting different profiles compared to general-purpose models. The paper argues that semantic fusion without conceptual grounding is a significant risk factor for hallucinations and that the HQP framework offers a reproducible way to quantify this phenomenon.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in its systematic approach to inducing and quantifying hallucinations.  While the idea of LLMs hallucinating isn't new, the introduction of HIPs as a specifically engineered stimulus and HQPs as a means for evaluation provides a structured framework. Other methods rely on inherent flaws or biases in the model or training data. The study's focus on semantic fusion as a key driver of hallucinations is a significant contribution, as it shifts the focus from mere factual inaccuracy to the breakdown of conceptual coherence.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is potentially high. Understanding and quantifying the conditions under which LLMs hallucinate is crucial for their safe and reliable deployment. The framework proposed by the authors could be used for:

    *   **Model Evaluation:** Identifying models that are more or less susceptible to prompt-induced hallucinations.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** Developing guidelines for designing prompts that minimize the risk of hallucinations.
    *   **Model Improvement:** Informing the development of methods for detecting and mitigating hallucinations.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Framework:** The HIP/HQP framework is clearly articulated and relatively simple to implement, which increases its potential for adoption by other researchers.
    *   **Controlled Experiments:** The experiments appear to be well-controlled, with careful attention paid to matching prompt length and structure between HIPs and control prompts.
    *   **Multi-Model Comparison:** The study included a range of LLMs, which helps to generalize the findings and identify model-specific vulnerabilities.
    *   **Emphasis on Semantic Fusion:** The focus on semantic fusion provides a deeper understanding of the underlying cognitive dynamics of hallucination.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Subjectivity of HQP Evaluation:**  Although the authors use an independent LLM (GPT-03) for HQP evaluation, the process still relies on a judgment of plausibility, confidence, and coherence, which can be somewhat subjective. Inter-rater reliability between different HQP evaluators (even automated ones) needs to be considered.
    *   **Limited Scope of Semantic Fusion:**  The paper focuses on a specific type of semantic fusion (periodic table and tarot). While this is a useful starting point, further research is needed to determine whether the findings generalize to other types of conceptual combinations.
    *   **HQP Model Choice and Stability:** Relying on a single model for the HQP raises questions regarding its inherent biases and potential instability across different versions or updates. The authors address using stateless evaluation, but ensuring consistency with HQP analysis is very important.
    *   **External Validation:** The HQP scores are an external measure of hallucinations and only indirectly suggest issues with model's epistemic integrity.
*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's framework could be a valuable tool for researchers and developers working to improve the reliability and safety of LLMs. It could also inform the development of better benchmarks and evaluation metrics for assessing hallucination risk.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper introduces a novel and potentially valuable framework, some limitations detract from the overall score. The potential for subjectivity in the HQP evaluation and the limited scope of the semantic fusion studied are areas that need to be addressed in future research. Although there is a reproducible study of hallucination with HIP/HQP framework, the framework focuses on a specific domain and some aspects in HQP scoring seems arbitrary.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### X-ray illicit object detection using hybrid CNN-transformer neural network architectures
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00564v1)
- **Authors**: Jorgen Cani, Christos Diou, Spyridon Evangelatos, Panagiotis Radoglou-Grammatikis, Vasileios Argyriou, Panagiotis Sarigiannidis, Iraklis Varlamis, Georgios Th. Papadopoulos
- **Abstract**: In the field of X-ray security applications, even the smallest details can significantly impact outcomes. Objects that are heavily occluded or intentionally concealed pose a great challenge for detection, whether by human observation or through advanced technological applications. While certain Deep Learning (DL) architectures demonstrate strong performance in processing local information, such as Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), others excel in handling distant information, e.g., transformers. In X-ray security imaging the literature has been dominated by the use of CNN-based methods, while the integration of the two aforementioned leading architectures has not been sufficiently explored. In this paper, various hybrid CNN-transformer architectures are evaluated against a common CNN object detection baseline, namely YOLOv8. In particular, a CNN (HGNetV2) and a hybrid CNN-transformer (Next-ViT-S) backbone are combined with different CNN/transformer detection heads (YOLOv8 and RT-DETR). The resulting architectures are comparatively evaluated on three challenging public X-ray inspection datasets, namely EDS, HiXray, and PIDray. Interestingly, while the YOLOv8 detector with its default backbone (CSP-DarkNet53) is generally shown to be advantageous on the HiXray and PIDray datasets, when a domain distribution shift is incorporated in the X-ray images (as happens in the EDS datasets), hybrid CNN-transformer architectures exhibit increased robustness. Detailed comparative evaluation results, including object-level detection performance and object-size error analysis, demonstrate the strengths and weaknesses of each architectural combination and suggest guidelines for future research. The source code and network weights of the models employed in this study are available at https://github.com/jgenc/xray-comparative-evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the application of hybrid CNN-transformer architectures for illicit object detection in X-ray security imaging. It compares several hybrid architectures (combining HGNetV2 or Next-ViT-S backbones with YOLOv8 or RT-DETR detection heads) against a standard CNN-based object detection baseline (YOLOv8 with CSP-DarkNet53) across three public X-ray datasets: EDS, HiXray, and PIDray.  The study analyzes object detection performance, object size-related errors, and robustness to domain shifts (simulated by the use of different X-ray scanners in the EDS dataset). The findings suggest that while CNN-only architectures perform well in datasets collected from single scanners, hybrid architectures exhibit improved robustness when dealing with domain shifts introduced by varying scanner characteristics. The YOLOv8 head generally outperforms the RT-DETR head.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addressing a Relevant Problem:** Illicit object detection in X-ray security is a vital and ongoing research area with practical applications.
    *   **Exploration of Hybrid Architectures:** The paper contributes by exploring hybrid CNN-transformer architectures, which are becoming increasingly important in various computer vision tasks, within the specific context of X-ray imaging, where CNN-based methods have traditionally dominated.
    *   **Comparative Evaluation:** The study provides a comprehensive comparative evaluation of several different architectures against a strong CNN baseline, which is crucial for understanding their relative strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Attention to Domain Shift:** Recognizing and addressing the domain shift problem in X-ray imaging, stemming from variations in scanner characteristics, is a significant and practical contribution. Using the EDS dataset to evaluate robustness to domain shifts adds value to the study.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The analysis includes object-level performance, object size error analysis, and illustrative detection results, providing a thorough understanding of the models' behavior.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Publicly available code and model weights enhance the reproducibility and usefulness of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Architecture:** The architectural combinations are relatively straightforward integrations of existing components (YOLOv8, RT-DETR, HGNetV2, Next-ViT-S).  The novelty mainly lies in their application to the X-ray domain and the analysis of their performance in this context. There's no new architectural component or significant modification to the existing ones.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The findings, while useful, are somewhat incremental. The improvement achieved by hybrid architectures in domain shift scenarios is modest rather than a breakthrough. The observation that the YOLOv8 head performs better than RT-DETR could be further explored, as it somewhat contradicts the trend of transformers performing superior to CNNs.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Hyperparameters:** Although stated in implementation details, some more discussion could have been dedicated to tuning hyperparameters. While batch size and learning rate are discussed, the scope for improvement in overall mAP is potentially limited by this factor.
    *   **Lack of Deep Dive into Transformer Benefits:** The paper highlights the increased robustness due to transformers' capability to incorporate global contextual information, but does not thoroughly investigate *why* and *how* the specific Next-ViT-S architecture achieves better robustness than a CNN-only one. Visualizations or further analyses quantifying the impact of global context modeling could strengthen the argument.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** While the paper deals with X-ray images, the problem of domain shift is discussed and accounted for. This might limit impact in other image domains.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's findings offer practical guidance for selecting appropriate architectures for X-ray security applications, particularly when dealing with data from multiple scanners.
    *   The work can serve as a baseline for future research in this area, encouraging the development of more robust and efficient hybrid CNN-transformer architectures for X-ray imaging.
    *   The open-source nature of the code and models promotes further experimentation and development by other researchers.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid, albeit somewhat incremental, contribution to the field. It systematically evaluates relevant architectures and highlights the importance of considering domain shift in X-ray security applications. While the novelty of the architectural combinations is limited, the thorough experimental evaluation and practical insights justify a score in the middle range. The paper is well-written and addresses a practically relevant problem, but lacks major breakthroughs or fundamentally new concepts.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### FreqKV: Frequency Domain Key-Value Compression for Efficient Context Window Extension
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.00570v1)
- **Authors**: Jushi Kai, Boyi Zeng, Yixuan Wang, Haoli Bai, Bo Jiang, Zhouhan Lin
- **Abstract**: Extending the context window in large language models (LLMs) is essential for applications involving long-form content generation. However, the linear increase in key-value (KV) cache memory requirements and the quadratic complexity of self-attention with respect to sequence length present significant challenges during fine-tuning and inference. Existing methods suffer from performance degradation when extending to longer contexts. In this work, we introduce a novel context extension method that optimizes both fine-tuning and inference efficiency. Our method exploits a key observation: in the frequency domain, the energy distribution of the KV cache is primarily concentrated in low-frequency components. By filtering out the high-frequency components, the KV cache can be effectively compressed with minimal information loss. Building on this insight, we propose an efficient compression technique, FreqKV, that iteratively compresses the increasing KV cache to a fixed size in the frequency domain, applicable to both fine-tuning and inference. FreqKV introduces no additional parameters or architectural modifications. With minimal fine-tuning, LLMs can learn to leverage the limited cache that is compressed in the frequency domain and extend the context window efficiently. Experiments on various long context language modeling and understanding tasks demonstrate the efficiency and efficacy of the proposed method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FreqKV, a method for compressing the key-value (KV) cache in large language models (LLMs) to extend the context window efficiently. The core idea is based on the observation that the energy distribution of the KV cache in the frequency domain is primarily concentrated in low-frequency components. FreqKV compresses the KV cache by filtering out high-frequency components, leading to minimal information loss.  The method is applicable to both fine-tuning and inference, introduces no additional parameters, and does not require architectural modifications. Experiments on language modeling and understanding tasks demonstrate the efficiency and effectiveness of the approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the application of frequency domain compression specifically to the KV cache of decoder-only LLMs for context extension. While frequency domain techniques have been used in computer vision and even on encoder layers in NLP (like FNet), adapting it to the autoregressive nature of decoder-only LLMs is a distinct contribution. The iterative compression approach tied to the context window size is also a clever design element. Prior KV compression methods focused on eviction, merging, or introducing additional compressor modules, whereas FreqKV provides a simpler, parameter-free approach.
* **Significance:** The ability to efficiently extend the context window of LLMs is of high practical significance. FreqKV addresses this challenge without introducing significant performance degradation or the need for extensive retraining. The fact that it works with minimal fine-tuning is a significant advantage.  The experimental results, showcasing comparable or superior performance to existing KV compression techniques on various tasks (especially long context modeling), further strengthens its significance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Simplicity:** FreqKV is relatively simple to implement and doesn't require architectural changes, making it easily adaptable to existing LLMs.
    * **Efficiency:** The method is efficient in terms of both memory usage and computation, demonstrated by the low compression overhead and the linear growth of decoding time.
    * **Effectiveness:** The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating good performance on language modeling and long-context understanding tasks.
    * **Parameter-free:** The method doesn't introduce new parameters, alleviating the memory burden.
* **Weaknesses:**
